=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer side, representing the edge length of a square with corners at (0, 0), (0, side), (side, 0), and (side, side) on a Cartesian plane.
You are also given a positive integer k and a 2D integer array points, where points[i] = [x_i, y_i] represents the coordinate of a point lying on the boundary of the square.
You need to select k elements among points such that the minimum Manhattan distance between any two points is maximized.
Return the maximum possible minimum Manhattan distance between the selected k points.
The Manhattan Distance between two cells (x_i, y_i) and (x_j, y_j) is |x_i - x_j| + |y_i - y_j|.
 
Example 1:

Input: side = 2, points = [[0,2],[2,0],[2,2],[0,0]], k = 4
Output: 2
Explanation:

Select all four points.

Example 2:

Input: side = 2, points = [[0,0],[1,2],[2,0],[2,2],[2,1]], k = 4
Output: 1
Explanation:

Select the points (0, 0), (2, 0), (2, 2), and (2, 1).

Example 3:

Input: side = 2, points = [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2],[1,2],[2,0],[2,2],[2,1]], k = 5
Output: 1
Explanation:

Select the points (0, 0), (0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 2), and (2, 2).

 
Constraints:

1 <= side <= 10^9
4 <= points.length <= min(4 * side, 15 * 10^3)
points[i] == [xi, yi]
The input is generated such that:
	
points[i] lies on the boundary of the square.
All points[i] are unique.


4 <= k <= min(25, points.length)

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxDistance(self, side: int, points: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        
```

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Can we prove L_s <= 2D? For opposite sides, L_s = 2S - |x1-x2|? Let's check x1=0,x2=10: 2S -10=10? But actual L_s=20. So no. Let's derive correct formula. Let a=x1, b=x2. L1 = S + |a-b|. Now use this to prove adjacent sufficiency.

Suppose we have selected points in circular order with all adjacent Manhattan distances >= D. Take any non-adjacent pair A,C. Let d = D(A,C). Need show d >= D. Assume d < D. Let L_s be shorter boundary arc length between A,C. We know L_s <= 2d < 2D. Since A,C are non-adjacent in the selected circular order, there is at least one selected point on each of the two boundary arcs between A,C? Wait in a circular order, if A,C are not adjacent, there is at least one selected point on one arc and at least one on the other? Actually in a cycle, two non-adjacent vertices divide the cycle into two paths, each containing at least one other selected vertex. Yes because if one arc had no selected vertices, then A and C would be consecutive in the circular order (the edge between them along that arc), making them adjacent. So both arcs contain selected points.

Consider the shorter arc γ between A,C (length L_s). It contains at least one selected point (since both arcs contain selected; if both arcs, yes). Let P be the first selected point encountered along γ from A to C, and Q be the last? The selected points on γ form a path from A to C: A = P0, P1, ..., Pm = C, with m>=1, where P_i are selected points in order along γ, and consecutive P_{i-1},P_i are adjacent in the selected cycle? Are they necessarily adjacent in the full selected cycle? Since there are no selected points between them on γ, but there could be selected points on the other arc between them? In circular order, P_{i-1} and P_i are consecutive along the entire circle if the other arc between them contains no selected? Wait the full circle is divided into two arcs between P_{i-1} and P_i: one is the subarc of γ with no selected points; the other is the complement, which contains all other selected points including A,C. Thus P_{i-1} and P_i are adjacent in the circular order (the edge along γ) because the subarc between them has no selected points. Yes, consecutive selected points in circular order are determined by arcs with no selected points; if the γ subarc has no selected points, then P_{i-1} and P_i are consecutive along that direction. So D(P_{i-1},P_i) >= D by assumption.

Now, for any two points on the same boundary arc γ, the Manhattan distance between them is <= the length of the subarc of γ between them? Is that always? The subarc is a path along the boundary consisting of axis-aligned segments; Manhattan distance is the length of the shortest axis-aligned path, so it is <= length of any axis-aligned path, including the boundary subarc. Yes.

Thus for each segment i, D(P_{i-1},P_i) <= length(subarc_i). Since D(P_{i-1},P_i) >= D, we have length(subarc_i) >= D. Summing over m segments gives L_s = sum length(subarc_i) >= m*D. Since m>=1. If m>=2, L_s >=2D. But we have L_s <=2d <2D, contradiction. If m=1, then there is exactly one selected point P on γ. Then D(A,P) >=D and D(P,C)>=D (adjacent edges along γ? Actually A-P and P-C are adjacent in selected cycle? If no other selected on γ, yes A and P are consecutive along γ, and P and C consecutive along γ, so distances >=D). Then L_s >= D(A,P)+D(P,C)? Wait subarc lengths: length(A,P) >= D(A,P) >=D, length(P,C) >=D(P,C)>=D, so L_s >=2D, contradiction. More generally m segments each length >=D, so L_s >= mD >=2D? If m=1, L_s = len(A,P)+len(P,C) >= D(A,P)+D(P,C) >=2D. So yes L_s >=2D. Contradiction with L_s <=2d<2D. Therefore d>=D. Great! This proves adjacent sufficiency.

Excellent! So we can reduce to selecting k points on the boundary circle such that the Manhattan distance between consecutive selected points in circular order is at least D. Because if adjacent distances >=D, then all pairwise >=D. Conversely pairwise >=D implies adjacent >=D. So decision is exactly existence of a k-cycle in the directed graph where edges from i to j (clockwise) exist if D(i,j)>=D, and we need choose k vertices around the circle with each consecutive edge valid. Let's derive the superlevel intervals explicitly.

For a fixed point u=(x,y) on boundary, and a candidate Q=(X,Y) on boundary, D = |x-X|+|y-Y|. Since both on boundary, we can classify Q by side. The condition D>=D on each side is linear in the side coordinate. The earliest feasible is the minimum perimeter coordinate among points on any side satisfying the linear inequality. We can find it by considering the four sides separately and taking the minimum p. For each side, the set of points on that side satisfying D>=D is an interval (possibly empty) in the side coordinate, because D as a function of the side coordinate is linear (absolute value with at most one kink). We can find the first point on that side after u with D>=D using binary search on the side's sorted points. Since there are only 4 sides, this is O(log n) per u! This is the key.

Yes! Instead of dealing with the whole perimeter, we can compute earliest feasible by checking each of the four sides. For a given u, for each side s, the points on side s are sorted by perimeter coordinate (which is monotonic along the side). The Manhattan distance from u to a point on side s as a function of the side coordinate is piecewise linear with at most one kink (because absolute value of difference in x or y). We can find the first point on side s (in perimeter order after u) with distance >=D by binary searching on the side's coordinate using a predicate that is not monotone? Let's check.

Example u=(5,0) bottom. Side top (s=2): points on top have x from S down to0, p=3S-x. Distance to top point (X,S) = |X-5|+S. As X decreases from S to0, |X-5| decreases from5 to0 then increases to5. So distance is V-shaped: high at X=S (15), low at X=5 (10), high at X=0 (15). The condition D>=12 is true for X<=3 or X>=7. In perimeter order p=30-X, as p increases (X decreases), distance decreases from15 to10 then increases to15. So the feasible set on this side is two intervals: p∈[20,23] (X 10..7) and p∈[27,30] (X 3..0). Not a single interval. Binary search on side coordinate for first feasible is not trivial, but we can find the first feasible by considering the two monotonic pieces around the kink. The kink is at the projection of u onto the side (same coordinate). For each side, the distance function is convex (V-shaped) with one minimum. The superlevel set is either one interval (if D <= min? actually outside a central interval) or two intervals near the ends. Specifically, condition |coord - a| + const >= D is equivalent to coord <= a - (D-const) OR coord >= a + (D-const), if D>const; if D<=const, all points on that side satisfy. So it's a union of at most two intervals in the side coordinate. We can find the first point in each interval by binary search on the sorted coordinates of that side. Then take the minimum perimeter coordinate among all sides and intervals, but only for points after u in the circular order (including duplicate for wrap). This gives earliest feasible in O(log n) per side interval.

Let's formalize.

We have points on boundary. We can group them by side:
- bottom: y=0, x∈[0,S], perimeter p = x.
- right: x=S, y∈[0,S], p = S + y.
- top: y=S, x∈[0,S], but perimeter p = 3S - x (clockwise from bottom-left via right/top). So as x increases, p decreases. We can store top points sorted by p (which is decreasing x) or by x.
- left: x=0, y∈[0,S], p = 4S - y (clockwise from bottom-left via right/top/left). As y increases, p decreases.

For a given point u=(x,y), and a candidate point q on side s with coordinate t along that side (let's define t as the natural coordinate along the side in the direction of increasing perimeter?):
- bottom: q=(t,0), p=t, t∈[0,S].
- right: q=(S,t), p=S+t, t∈[0,S].
- top: q=(t,S) where t=x coordinate, p=3S-t. To have p increasing, t decreases. We can store top points sorted by p, but for distance formula use t=x.
- left: q=(0,t) where t=y coordinate, p=4S-t. Store sorted by p, distance formula uses t=y.

For each side, we need find the first point (by perimeter p) after u (p > p_u, or p + 4S for wrap) that satisfies D(u,q)>=D. Since we will duplicate points for circular processing, we can instead for each side find candidates with p > p_u and also p+4S > p_u (which is always for p>=0, but if p < p_u, the duplicate p+4S is after). To find earliest overall, we can consider all points on that side with p > p_u, and if none, consider the first point on that side with p+4S (duplicate) after p_u. But since we only need the earliest after u in the full circular order, we can search in the side's sorted list by p and if no candidate after, wrap to the beginning of the list with p+4S.

However, for a fixed side, the condition D>=D as a function of the natural coordinate t may have two intervals. We need find the minimum p among points in those intervals that are after p_u (including wrap). We can do this by finding the first point in each interval in the circular order.

Let's define for each side a list of points sorted by perimeter p. For a query u and side s, we need find the minimum p' in the circular order after p_u such that the point at p' (possibly duplicate p'+4S) has distance >=D. Equivalently, consider the set of original points on side s satisfying D>=D. For each such point with original p, if p > p_u, candidate p; if p < p_u, candidate p+4S (if p=p_u impossible unique). The earliest is the minimum of these candidates. We can find it by:
- Determine the intervals of t (natural coordinate) where D>=D.
- For each interval, find the corresponding range of original p values. Since p is monotonic in t (either increasing for bottom/right, decreasing for top/left), the interval in t maps to an interval in p (possibly reversed).
- We need the first point in that p-interval with p > p_u, or if none, the first point in the interval with p+4S (i.e., the smallest p in the interval) after wrap.
This can be done with binary search on the side's p-sorted list.

But we can simplify by using the side's sorted points by p and a custom predicate? Since the feasible set on a side may be two p-intervals, we can just compute the two p-intervals and query.

Let's derive distance formulas for each side.

Let u=(x,y).

1. Bottom side (B): q=(t,0), t∈[0,S]. D = |x - t| + y. (since |y-0|=y). Note if u is also on bottom, y=0.
Condition: |x - t| >= D - y.
Let R = D - y.
- If R <= 0: all t∈[0,S] feasible.
- Else: feasible t ∈ [0, x - R] ∪ [x + R, S] (intersect with [0,S]).
p = t.

2. Right side (R): q=(S,t), t∈[0,S]. D = |S - x| + |t - y| = (S - x) + |t - y| (since x<=S). Let C = S - x.
Condition: |t - y| >= D - C.
Let R = D - C.
- If R <= 0: all t feasible.
- Else: feasible t ∈ [0, y - R] ∪ [y + R, S].
p = S + t.

3. Top side (T): q=(t,S), t∈[0,S] (x-coordinate). D = |x - t| + |S - y| = |x - t| + (S - y). Let C = S - y.
Condition: |x - t| >= D - C.
Let R = D - C.
- If R <= 0: all t feasible.
- Else: feasible t ∈ [0, x - R] ∪ [x + R, S].
p = 3S - t. (So p decreases as t increases.)

4. Left side (L): q=(0,t), t∈[0,S] (y-coordinate). D = x + |y - t|. Let C = x.
Condition: |y - t| >= D - x.
Let R = D - x.
- If R <= 0: all t feasible.
- Else: feasible t ∈ [0, y - R] ∪ [y + R, S].
p = 4S - t. (p decreases as t increases.)

Note: If u itself lies on the side being queried, the point u may be in the feasible set if D=0? But D>=D with D>0, so u not feasible (distance 0). The intervals might include t=x or t=y; we need exclude the point with p equal to p_u? Since points are unique and D>0, the condition will not include u if R>0? For same side, e.g bottom u=(x,0), D to bottom q = |x-t|. Condition |x-t|>=D. Intervals t<=x-D or t>=x+D, so u not included. If R<=0 (D<=0) not possible since D positive. For side not containing u, no issue. So no need to exclude.

Now, given intervals in t, we need map to p intervals and query the side list.

For bottom/right: p = t or S+t, increasing in t. An interval t ∈ [l,r] maps to p ∈ [l, S+l? actually for bottom p=t, right p=S+t]. So p interval [p_l, p_r] increasing.

For top/left: p = 3S - t or 4S - t, decreasing in t. An interval t ∈ [l,r] maps to p ∈ [3S - r, 3S - l] (since larger t -> smaller p). Similarly left [4S - r, 4S - l].

We need find the first point in the side's sorted p-list within a p-interval [L,R] (inclusive) that is after p_u in circular order. Since p-list sorted ascending. The candidates are:
- Points with p in [L,R] and p > p_u: the first is lower_bound(p_list, max(L, p_u+epsilon))? Since p are integers? Coordinates integers, p integers. p_u is integer. Need p > p_u, so use bisect_right(p_list, p_u) to get first index with p > p_u, then check if <= upper_bound(R).
- If no such point, then points with p in [L,R] and p <= p_u can be used via wrap: their duplicate p+4S. The earliest after p_u among them is the smallest p in [L,R] (since p+4S, smaller p gives earlier duplicate). Actually if p <= p_u, candidate p+4S. The minimum p+4S is min p in [L,R] +4S. So we can take the first point in the interval overall (lowest p) and add 4S. But wait if there is a point with p > p_u, its p is < p_u+4S, so it's earlier than any wrapped point (p+4S >4S > p_u+? Actually p_u <4S, p+4S >4S > p_u, but a non-wrapped p > p_u could be up to 4S, which is less than 4S+min p? Since min p>=0, p+4S >=4S, and non-wrapped p <4S. So non-wrapped candidates are always earlier than wrapped candidates. Thus we only wrap if no non-wrapped candidate in the interval. Good.

So for each feasible p-interval, we can find the earliest candidate after p_u as:
idx = bisect_right(p_list, p_u)  # first p > p_u
if idx < len and p_list[idx] <= R:
    candidate = p_list[idx]
else:
    # no non-wrapped; use first point in interval wrapped
    idx2 = bisect_left(p_list, L)
    if idx2 < len and p_list[idx2] <= R:
        candidate = p_list[idx2] + 4*S
    else:
        no candidate
But careful: If p_u is inside the interval and there are points after p_u, idx gives first after. If no points after but there are points before/equal, wrap. If p_u is before interval, idx may be first in interval, candidate non-wrapped. If p_u is after interval, idx > R, no non-wrapped; wrap first in interval. Good.

We need do this for each feasible t-interval (up to 2) on each side (4 sides), so up to 8 queries per u. Each query uses bisect on a list of p values for that side. O(8 log n) per u. For n=15000, k=25, binary search 31: 15000*8*log*25*31 ~ 15000*8*14*25*31 = 1.3e9 bisect operations too high. But we can optimize.

Let's think of a more efficient way to find earliest feasible for a given u and D using the four sides but without 8 bisects. We can compute the first feasible point overall by considering each side's feasible intervals and taking min. We can reduce intervals:

This is a huge optimization. For each decision D:
- Compute nxt[i] for each point i (0..n-1): the index j (in duplicated/original sense) of the earliest point after i in circular order with Manhattan distance >= D. Can we compute nxt[i] more efficiently using side interval queries? Yes.

Let's define side lists:
We have n points. We'll assign each point a side 0..3:
0 bottom: y==0, p=x, coord=x.
1 right: x==S, p=S+y, coord=y.
2 top: y==S, p=3*S - x, coord=x.
3 left: x==0, p=4*S - y, coord=y.
Note corners belong to two sides. We need assign each point to exactly one side to avoid duplicates in side lists. For uniqueness, we can assign by priority: bottom if y==0; else right if x==S; else top if y==S; else left. This assigns corners: (0,0) bottom, (S,0) bottom, (S,S) right, (0,S) top? Wait (0,S) y==S, x=0, priority bottom no, right no, top yes -> top. (S,S) right before top -> right. This is fine; each point appears once. But for distance queries to a side, if a corner is assigned to one side, it won't be considered as part of the other side. Is that okay? Points are unique; if a point lies at a corner, it belongs to both sides geometrically, but for selection it's one point. For finding earliest feasible, if the point is at a corner, it should be considered once. Assigning to one side is fine because we only need each point once. However, when querying a side, if a corner point is assigned to another side, we might miss it as a candidate on that side. But it will be considered on its assigned side. Since the point is unique, that's sufficient. The distance formula for a corner works for either side; but if we assign (S,S) to right, it won't be in top list. When querying top side, we won't see (S,S), but it will be seen when querying right side. The earliest feasible after u might be (S,S); if it's assigned to right, the right side query will find it. Good.

So side lists partition points.

For each side, store:
- pts: list of point indices sorted by p ascending.
- p_list: corresponding p values.
- coord_list: natural coordinate t for each point (same order as p? For bottom/right, p increases with t, so coord_list ascending. For top/left, p decreases with t, so coord_list descending if sorted by p. We'll need to query intervals in t and map to p. We can store p_list and coord_list aligned with p order. For top, p=3S-t, so as p increases, t decreases. Thus coord_list is descending. For left, p=4S-t, coord descending.

To find candidates on a side given t-intervals [l,r], we need find p interval and then bisect in p_list. We can write a helper query_side(side_idx, l, r, p_u) that returns the earliest duplicated index after p_u with coordinate t in [l,r] (inclusive), or INF.

Steps:
- Clamp l,r to [0,S]. If l>r, no interval.
- Compute p_L, p_R for the interval:
  if side in (bottom, right): p_L = base + l, p_R = base + r, where base=0 for bottom, S for right.
  if side in (top, left): p_L = base - r, p_R = base - l, where base=3S for top, 4S for left. (Since p=base - t, t∈[l,r] => p∈[base-r, base-l]).
- Now we need first point in this side's p_list within [p_L, p_R] that is after p_u in circular order.
  Use bisect_right on p_list for p_u to get idx_start (first p > p_u).
  If idx_start < len and p_list[idx_start] <= p_R:
      j = side_pts[idx_start]
      # candidate p = p_list[idx_start] (non-wrapped). Since p > p_u, duplicated index is j (0..n-1).
      return j (as duplicated index? original index)
  Else:
      # no non-wrapped; use first point in interval wrapped
      idx2 = bisect_left(p_list, p_L)
      if idx2 < len and p_list[idx2] <= p_R:
          j = side_pts[idx2]
          # candidate duplicated index = j + n (since p + L > p_u)
          return j + n
      else:
          return INF
But careful: If p_u is less than p_L, idx_start may point to first point in interval, non-wrapped. Good. If p_u is inside interval, idx_start points to first after p_u, non-wrapped. If p_u is greater than p_R, idx_start > R, so wrap first point. If p_u is less than p_L but there are no points in interval? idx_start maybe first point >=p_L, check <=p_R. Good.

We need ensure we don't return the start duplicate? In nxt computation, p_u is current point; we are computing global earliest after current, not tied to a start. If the candidate is the same point as current via wrap? Since p > p_u, non-wrapped candidate cannot be current (p_current not > p_current). Wrapped candidate p+L > p_u always, and if j == current, then p+L is the duplicate of current; distance 0 <D, so not feasible. Our interval condition D>=D will not include current because D>0, so j==current won't satisfy. Thus no issue.

Now, for a given point u and D, we need compute nxt[u] = minimum over all sides and their feasible intervals of the candidate duplicated index. We can compute by iterating over sides 0..3, computing feasible t intervals, and querying. But we can optimize by precomputing for each point u its coordinates and side.

Let's write a function get_intervals(u, side, D):
Given u=(x,y), side s, return list of (l,r) t-intervals on that side where D(u, q) >= D.

Use formulas above. Let's define S = side length.

For side 0 (bottom): q=(t,0), D = abs(x-t) + y.
R = D - y.
if R <= 0: intervals = [(0,S)]
else:
  intervals = []
  l1=0; r1=x-R; if l1<=r1 and r1>=0 and l1<=S: clamp -> (max(0,l1), min(S,r1))
  l2=x+R; r2=S; if l2<=r2 and l2<=S: clamp -> (max(0,l2), min(S,r2))
Need handle R maybe >S, intervals empty.

For side 1 (right): q=(S,t), D = (S-x) + abs(t-y).
C = S - x
R = D - C
if R <=0: all
else intervals around y.

For side 2 (top): q=(t,S), D = abs(x-t) + (S-y).
C = S - y
R = D - C
if R <=0: all
else intervals around x.

For side 3 (left): q=(0,t), D = x + abs(y-t).
C = x
R = D - C
if R <=0: all
else intervals around y.

Note: If u is on the side being queried, the distance formula includes the coordinate of u; e.g u on bottom y=0, side bottom R=D, intervals t<=x-D or t>=x+D. Good.

Now, we also need to compute D(u, v) for closing check and for maybe verifying nxt. We can compute Manhattan directly.

I think the algorithm is likely correct. Let's proceed.

Need implement binary search:
lo=0, hi=2*S (max Manhattan distance between any two points in square is 2S). While lo<hi: mid=(lo+hi+1)//2; if feasible(mid): lo=mid else hi=mid-1. Return lo.

Feasible(D):
- If D==0: return True (k>=4 but okay).
- Compute nxt array of length n? Actually we need nxt for original indices 0..n-1, but during simulation current may become a duplicated index j+n (>=n). We need nxt for duplicated indices too. We can either compute nxt for all 2n duplicated indices, or when current >= n, map to original j and adjust? nxt[j+n] should be the earliest feasible after p_j+L. Since the pattern repeats every L, nxt[j+n] = nxt[j] + n (if nxt[j]!=-1). Because distances depend only on original points and the circular order shifted by L. So we can compute nxt for original indices 0..n-1, and for cur>=n, use nxt[cur-n] + n. But careful: nxt[j] is earliest after original j in the original circle (could be a wrapped index k+n). If we shift by L, nxt[j+n] = nxt[j] + n. Yes.
- Simulation:
  for s in range(n):
      cur = s
      cnt = 1
      ok = True
      while cnt < k:
          nxt_idx = nxt[cur] if cur < n else (nxt[cur-n] + n if nxt[cur-n] != -1 else -1)
          if nxt_idx == -1 or nxt_idx >= s + n:
              ok = False; break
          cur = nxt_idx
          cnt += 1
      if ok and manhattan(points[cur % n], points[s]) >= D:
          return True
  return False

Wait, if cur >= n, cur % n gives original point. nxt[cur] = nxt[cur-n]+n. The end boundary s+n is in duplicated index space. Since we start at s (0..n-1), all selected intermediate indices must be < s+n. If nxt_idx >= s+n, stop. If nxt_idx == s+n (start duplicate), stop. Good.

But there is a subtlety: The greedy path might select a point that is before the start in original order via wrap (index j+n with j < s). That's allowed as an intermediate if j+n < s+n, i.e., j < s. It corresponds to a point after start in circular order. Good.

When checking closing, current may be >=n; original index cur % n. Manhattan distance between original points.

Now, computing nxt for original i:
We need consider candidates after p_i in circular order. Our query_side returns duplicated index j (if non-wrapped) or j+n (if wrapped). The minimum over all intervals/sides gives nxt[i]. But we must ensure the candidate is not the same as i (distance 0). Since D>0, no.

Let's implement query_side efficiently.

We'll have side_data:
For each side s:
  pts: list of point indices
  pvals: list of p values sorted ascending
  coords: list of t values aligned with pvals (for top/left, descending)
  base: 0 for bottom, S for right, 3S for top, 4S for left
  increasing: True for bottom/right, False for top/left

But for query, we don't actually need coords list except maybe for intervals? We compute intervals in t and map to p using base. We need pvals and pts.

Function query_interval(side, l, r, p_u):
  if l < 0: l=0; if r > S: r=S; if l>r: return INF
  if side in (0,1):
      pL = base + l
      pR = base + r
  else:
      pL = base - r
      pR = base - l
  # pL <= pR
  idx = bisect_right(pvals, p_u)  # first p > p_u
  if idx < len(pvals) and pvals[idx] <= pR:
      # Since idx is first > p_u, and pvals sorted, pvals[idx] >= pL? Not necessarily if p_u < pL, idx is first >= p_u, could be < pL? If p_u < pL, first > p_u could be before pL? Actually if p_u < pL, bisect_right returns first index with p > p_u, which could be an element with p < pL if there are points between p_u and pL. But if p < pL, it's not in the interval. We need ensure pvals[idx] >= pL. Since idx is first > p_u, and pL > p_u, the first element >= pL is at or after idx. So checking pvals[idx] <= pR is not enough; pvals[idx] might be < pL. Example p_u=0, pL=10, pvals=[5,15], bisect_right returns index0 (5), 5<=pR but not in interval. We need use bisect_left(pvals, pL) to get first p >= pL, then check if <=pR and >p_u. But if p_u is inside interval, bisect_left(pL) gives first >=pL, which is <=p_u maybe; we need first >p_u. So:

      if idx < len and pvals[idx] <= pR and pvals[idx] > p_u:
          return pts[idx]
      # else no non-wrapped? Wait if the first point >=pL is <=p_u, there might be a later point >p_u in interval. We should find first >p_u:

          return pts[idx]
  Actually simpler:

          return pts[idx]

      if idx < len and pvals[idx] <= pR:
          return pts[idx]
  This works: start at first p>=pL; if it's <=p_u, jump to first p>p_u; then if <=pR, it's the first in interval after p_u.
  If no non-wrapped, wrap:

We can combine:
def first_in_interval(pvals, pts, pL, pR, p_u):
    idx = bisect_left(pvals, pL)
    if idx < len and pvals[idx] <= pR:
        if pvals[idx] > p_u:
            return pts[idx]  # non-wrapped
        # there is at least one point in interval, but first is <=p_u
        idx2 = bisect_right(pvals, p_u)
        if idx2 < len and pvals[idx2] <= pR:
            return pts[idx2]  # non-wrapped after p_u
        # no non-wrapped after p_u; wrap the first point in interval
        return pts[idx] + n
    return INF
But what if the first point in interval is <=p_u, and there is no point >p_u in interval, but there is a point before pL? No, first in interval is the smallest p in interval. Wrapping it gives the earliest duplicate after p_u because all points in interval have p <= p_u, so their duplicates are p+L; the smallest p gives smallest duplicate. Good.

If the first point in interval is >p_u, return it non-wrapped. If there is no point in interval, INF.

Need ensure p_u is an integer p of current point. For current duplicated index cur>=n, p_u = p[cur % n] + L. In nxt computation for original i, p_u = p[i]. We only compute nxt for original i, so p_u < L. Good.

Pseudo:
def build_nxt(D):
    nxt = [-1]*n
    INF = 10**30
    for i in range(n):
        x=xs[i]; y=ys[i]; p_i=ps[i]
        best = INF
        # side 0
        R = D - y
        if R <= 0:
            cand = first_in_interval(side0, 0, S, p_i)
            best = min(best, cand)
        else:
            # interval1 [0, x-R]
            l=0; r=x-R
            if l <= r:
                cand = first_in_interval(side0, max(l,0), min(r,S), p_i)
                best = min(best, cand)
            # interval2 [x+R, S]
            l=x+R; r=S
            if l <= r:
                cand = first_in_interval(side0, max(l,0), min(r,S), p_i)
                best = min(best, cand)
        # side 1
        C = S - x
        R = D - C
        if R <= 0: interval all
        else intervals around y: [0, y-R], [y+R, S]
        # side 2
        C = S - y
        R = D - C
        if R <= 0: all
        else intervals around x
        # side 3
        C = x
        R = D - x
        if R <= 0: all
        else intervals around y
        if best < INF:
            nxt[i] = best
    return nxt

But wait: For side 1 (right), C = S - x. If u is on right, x=S, C=0. Good.
For side 3 (left), C = x. If u on left, x=0.

Now, let's think about the correctness of the greedy-from-every-start algorithm more. We should include a proof in final? The final answer only code, but we need be confident. I'll search memory: There is a known algorithm for "maximum minimum distance on a circle" where you binary search and for each starting point, greedily pick the next point at distance >= mid. This is correct if the distance function satisfies the "circular" property we proved (adjacent sufficiency). The proof: If there is a feasible set of k points, consider the feasible set that minimizes the sum of perimeter positions (lexicographically) among all feasible sets of size k. Then for its first point s, the second point must be the earliest point at distance >=D from s; otherwise replace with earlier point, maintaining feasibility by adjacent sufficiency? This is a standard exchange argument. Let's try to formulate:

Assume there exists a feasible k-set. Among all feasible k-sets, choose one with the lexicographically smallest sorted perimeter coordinates (or smallest sum). Let it be S = {s_0 < s_1 < ... < s_{k-1}} (circular). Consider the greedy sequence starting at s_0: g_0=s_0, g_1=nxt(s_0), ... . We want show g_i <= s_i for all i (in circular order) and g_{k-1} can close to s_0? Actually if g_1 < s_1, then replacing s_1 with g_1 in S yields a feasible set? Need check edges: s_0-g_1 valid; g_1-s_2? We need D(g_1, s_2) >=D. Since g_1 is before s_1 and D(s_1,s_2)>=D. Is D(g_1,s_2)>=D? Not guaranteed (our counterexample with p? p=(0,8), s=(5,0), g=(10,7), s_2=w? D(g,s_2)=3<D). So replacement may fail. But maybe choose the feasible set that is "greedy" by definition; existence of a feasible set implies the greedy algorithm from some start works, not necessarily from the lexicographically smallest.

Another exchange: Start from any feasible set. If for some consecutive pair (a,b) in the set, there is an earlier point c between them with D(a,c)>=D, then:
- If D(c,b)>=D, insert c (larger set), then remove some other point? Since any subset of a larger feasible set is feasible, we can get another k-set with smaller lexicographic order.
- If D(c,b)<D, then consider the set with a removed? We get a (k-1)-set. Not enough.
This is complex.

Given time, I'll trust the greedy-from-every-start algorithm because it's common and our tests support it. But let's see if there is a known counterexample for greedy on a circle with arbitrary distances. Suppose distance matrix is such that a valid cycle A->C->D->A exists, but nxt from A is B, from B is C, from C is D, from D is A? Then greedy from A would A,B,C,D? If B->C valid, then A,B,C,D is a valid 4-cycle (if D->A valid), so okay. If B->C invalid, then nxt from B might skip C. In our counterexample, nxt from A was B, B->A (close), so A failed, but C worked. Maybe the greedy path from some vertex in the cycle will follow the cycle.

I will proceed.

Let's implement build_nxt(D):
```
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right
INF = 10**30
nxt = [-1]*n
for i in range(n):
    x = xs[i]; y = ys[i]; p_u = ps[i]
    best = INF
    # side 0
    R = D - y
    if R <= 0:
        cand = first_in_interval(0, 0, S, p_u)
        if cand < best: best = cand
    else:
        l = 0; r = x - R
        if l <= r:
            cand = first_in_interval(0, max(l,0), min(r,S), p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        l = x + R; r = S
        if l <= r:
            cand = first_in_interval(0, max(l,0), min(r,S), p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
    # side 1
    C = S - x
    R = D - C
    if R <= 0:
        cand = first_in_interval(1, 0, S, p_u)
        ...
    else:
        intervals around y
    # side 2
    C = S - y
    R = D - C
    ...
    # side 3
    C = x
    R = D - x
    ...
    if best < INF: nxt[i] = best
```
Need clamp l,r properly. Since x,y in [0,S], R may be negative. If R<=0 all. If R>0, intervals:
[0, x-R] and [x+R, S]. The lower can be negative, upper >S; clamp. We can just call first_in_interval with l,r and let it clamp internally? Better clamp in first_in_interval:
```
if r < 0 or l > S or l > r: return INF
l = max(l,0); r = min(r,S)
if l > r: return INF
```
Then no need max/min at call. But if R<=0 all.

Let's implement first_in_interval(side, l, r, p_u):
```
if l < 0: l = 0
if r > S: r = S
if l > r: return INF
if side == 0:
    pL = l; pR = r
elif side == 1:
    pL = S + l; pR = S + r
elif side == 2:
    pL = 3*S - r; pR = 3*S - l
else:
    pL = 4*S - r; pR = 4*S - l
pvals = side_pvals[side]; pts = side_pts[side]
idx = bisect_left(pvals, pL)
if idx < len(pvals) and pvals[idx] <= pR:
    if pvals[idx] > p_u:
        return pts[idx]
    idx2 = bisect_right(pvals, p_u)
    if idx2 < len(pvals) and pvals[idx2] <= pR:
        return pts[idx2]
    # wrap
    return pts[idx] + n
return INF
```
Wait, if idx points to first p>=pL and pvals[idx] <= p_u, but there is a later point >p_u in interval, idx2 returns it. If idx2 returns a point, it's non-wrapped. If no later point, wrap the first point in interval. But what if the first point in interval is >p_u? We return it. Good.

But consider p_u > pR. idx = bisect_left(pL) gives first >=pL, which is <=pR (since interval nonempty) and <=p_u. idx2 = bisect_right(p_u) gives first >p_u, which is >pR, so no non-wrapped. Wrap first point. Good.
Consider p_u < pL. idx = first >=pL, >p_u, return non-wrapped. Good.
Consider p_u inside interval. idx = first >=pL, could be <=p_u. idx2 = first >p_u, if <=pR return non-wrapped; else wrap first. Good.

One subtlety: If the first point in interval is <=p_u, but there is a point >p_u in interval, we return it. If there is no point >p_u, we wrap the first point in interval. But what if the first point in interval is not the earliest wrapped? All points in interval have p <= p_u, so their duplicates are p+L. The smallest p gives smallest duplicate, so first point in interval is correct. Good.

Now, when computing nxt[i], p_u = ps[i]. The candidate returned could be pts[idx] (original index) or pts[idx]+n. Since pts are original sorted indices 0..n-1. The duplicated index order: original indices 0..n-1 correspond to p sorted; indices n..2n-1 correspond to p+L. If candidate is original index j, its duplicated index is j (since j's p > p_u and j > i? Actually if p_j > p_i, then j > i in sorted order, so duplicated index j is correct. If p_j > p_i but j could be < i? No, sorted by p, so p_j > p_i implies j > i. Good.)
If candidate is wrapped j+n, duplicated index j+n.

Now, feasible(D):
```
def feasible(D):
    if D == 0: return True
    nxt = build_nxt(D)
    for s in range(n):
        cur = s
        cnt = 1
        end = s + n
        while cnt < k:
            if cur < n:
                nxt_idx = nxt[cur]
            else:
                base_idx = cur - n
                nxt_idx = nxt[base_idx] + n if nxt[base_idx] != -1 else -1
            if nxt_idx == -1 or nxt_idx >= end:
                break
            cur = nxt_idx
            cnt += 1
        if cnt == k:
            # check closing
            if manhattan(xs[cur % n], ys[cur % n], xs[s], ys[s]) >= D:
                return True
    return False
```

Let's write first_in_interval as a nested function inside feasible or maxDistance? It needs side_pvals, side_pts, S, n. Define inside maxDistance to capture.

```
def first_in_interval(side, l, r, p_u):
    if l < 0: l = 0
    if r > S: r = S
    if l > r: return INF
    if side == 0:
        pL = l; pR = r
    elif side == 1:
        pL = S + l; pR = S + r
    elif side == 2:
        pL = 3*S - r; pR = 3*S - l
    else:
        pL = 4*S - r; pR = 4*S - l
    pvals = side_pvals[side]
    pts = side_pts[side]
    idx = bisect_left(pvals, pL)
    if idx < len(pvals) and pvals[idx] <= pR:
        if pvals[idx] > p_u:
            return pts[idx]
        idx2 = bisect_right(pvals, p_u)
        if idx2 < len(pvals) and pvals[idx2] <= pR:
            return pts[idx2]
        return pts[idx] + n
    return INF
```
Need ensure if idx == len, no interval. If idx points to p < pL? bisect_left ensures >=pL. Good.

Now, build_nxt:
```
def build_nxt(D):
    nxt = [-1]*n
    for i in range(n):
        x = xs[i]; y = ys[i]; p_u = ps[i]
        best = INF
        # side 0
        R = D - y
        if R <= 0:
            cand = first_in_interval(0, 0, S, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        else:
            cand = first_in_interval(0, 0, x - R, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
            cand = first_in_interval(0, x + R, S, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        # side 1
        C = S - x
        R = D - C
        if R <= 0:
            cand = first_in_interval(1, 0, S, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        else:
            cand = first_in_interval(1, 0, y - R, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
            cand = first_in_interval(1, y + R, S, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        # side 2
        C = S - y
        R = D - C
        if R <= 0:
            cand = first_in_interval(2, 0, S, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        else:
            cand = first_in_interval(2, 0, x - R, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
            cand = first_in_interval(2, x + R, S, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        # side 3
        C = x
        R = D - C
        if R <= 0:
            cand = first_in_interval(3, 0, S, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        else:
            cand = first_in_interval(3, 0, y - R, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
            cand = first_in_interval(3, y + R, S, p_u)
            if cand < best: best = cand
        if best < INF:
            nxt[i] = best
    return nxt
```
Note: For intervals like [0, x-R], if x-R <0, first_in_interval clamps l=0, r<0 -> l>r returns INF. Good. For [x+R, S], if x+R > S, l>S, r=S -> l>r INF. Good.

But wait: For side 0 bottom, the distance formula D = |x-t| + y. If R = D - y. If R <=0, all t feasible. If R >0, intervals t <= x-R OR t >= x+R. Correct.
For side 1 right, D = (S-x) + |t-y|. C=S-x. R=D-C. If R<=0 all. Intervals t <= y-R OR t >= y+R. Correct.
For side 2 top, D = |x-t| + (S-y). C=S-y. Intervals around x. Correct.
For side 3 left, D = x + |y-t|. C=x. Intervals around y. Correct.

Now, feasible:
```
def feasible(D):
    if D == 0: return True
    nxt = build_nxt(D)
    for s in range(n):
        cur = s
        cnt = 1
        end = s + n
        while cnt < k:
            if cur < n:
                nxt_idx = nxt[cur]
            else:
                ni = nxt[cur - n]
                nxt_idx = ni + n if ni != -1 else -1
            if nxt_idx == -1 or nxt_idx >= end:
                break
            cur = nxt_idx
            cnt += 1
        if cnt == k:
            if abs(xs[cur % n] - xs[s]) + abs(ys[cur % n] - ys[s]) >= D:
                return True
    return False
```
Potential issue: The greedy path may select the same point twice? Since nxt_idx strictly increases in duplicated index, and we stop before end=s+n, all selected indices are strictly increasing from s to <s+n, so no repeats. Good.

Now, binary search:
```
lo, hi = 0, 2*S
while lo < hi:
    mid = (lo + hi + 1)//2
    if feasible(mid):
        lo = mid
    else:
        hi = mid - 1
return lo
```
If S up to 1e9, 2S up to 2e9, 31 iterations.

If points length >4, maybe min distance could be 1? side>=1, unique integer coordinates on boundary, min Manhattan distance at least1? Points could be adjacent along boundary with distance1, so answer at least1 if k<=n? Since k<=n, can choose all points? If k=n and points include adjacent, answer1. So answer>=1. But binary search lo=0 fine.

No, if duplicated index < end, it's before the start duplicate, so okay. If it's the start duplicate itself, index == end, we stop. Good.

Now, one more potential issue: The greedy algorithm from every start using nxt may select a point that is not the earliest feasible *before end* if the global earliest feasible is after end but there is another feasible before end? Impossible: global earliest is the minimum over all feasible; if it's after end, all feasible are after end. If global earliest is before end, it's the earliest before end. So nxt is sufficient.

from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maxDistance(self, side: int, points: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        S = side
        n = len(points)
        L = 4*S
        # sort points by perimeter coordinate
        arr = []
        for idx, (x,y) in enumerate(points):
            if y == 0:
                p = x
                s = 0
            elif x == S:
                p = S + y
                s = 1
            elif y == S:
                p = 3*S - x
                s = 2
            else: # x == 0
                p = 4*S - y
                s = 3
            arr.append((p, x, y, s))
        arr.sort(key=lambda t: t[0])
        ps = [a[0] for a in arr]
        xs = [a[1] for a in arr]
        ys = [a[2] for a in arr]
        sides = [a[3] for a in arr]
        side_pts = [[] for _ in range(4)]
        side_pvals = [[] for _ in range(4)]
        for i in range(n):
            s = sides[i]
            side_pts[s].append(i)
            side_pvals[s].append(ps[i])
        INF = 10**30
        ...

Final code:

```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maxDistance(self, side: int, points: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        S = side
        n = len(points)
        L = 4 * S

        arr = []
        for x, y in points:
            if y == 0:
                p = x
                s = 0
            elif x == S:
                p = S + y
                s = 1
            elif y == S:
                p = 3 * S - x
                s = 2
            else:  # x == 0
                p = 4 * S - y
                s = 3
            arr.append((p, x, y, s))
        arr.sort(key=lambda t: t[0])

        ps = [a[0] for a in arr]
        xs = [a[1] for a in arr]
        ys = [a[2] for a in arr]
        side_id = [a[3] for a in arr]

        side_pts = [[] for _ in range(4)]
        side_pvals = [[] for _ in range(4)]
        for i in range(n):
            s = side_id[i]
            side_pts[s].append(i)
            side_pvals[s].append(ps[i])

        INF = 10**30

        def first_in_interval(side, l, r, p_u):
            if l < 0:
                l = 0
            if r > S:
                r = S
            if l > r:
                return INF
            if side == 0:
                pL, pR = l, r
            elif side == 1:
                pL, pR = S + l, S + r
            elif side == 2:
                pL, pR = 3 * S - r, 3 * S - l
            else:
                pL, pR = 4 * S - r, 4 * S - l

            pvals = side_pvals[side]
            pts = side_pts[side]
            idx = bisect_left(pvals, pL)
            if idx < len(pvals) and pvals[idx] <= pR:
                if pvals[idx] > p_u:
                    return pts[idx]
                idx2 = bisect_right(pvals, p_u)
                if idx2 < len(pvals) and pvals[idx2] <= pR:
                    return pts[idx2]
                return pts[idx] + n
            return INF

        def build_nxt(D):
            nxt = [-1] * n
            for i in range(n):
                x = xs[i]
                y = ys[i]
                p_u = ps[i]
                best = INF

                # bottom
                R = D - y
                if R <= 0:
                    cand = first_in_interval(0, 0, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand
                else:
                    cand = first_in_interval(0, 0, x - R, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand
                    cand = first_in_interval(0, x + R, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand

                # right
                C = S - x
                R = D - C
                if R <= 0:
                    cand = first_in_interval(1, 0, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand
                else:
                    cand = first_in_interval(1, 0, y - R, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand
                    cand = first_in_interval(1, y + R, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand

                # top
                C = S - y
                R = D - C
                if R <= 0:
                    cand = first_in_interval(2, 0, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand
                else:
                    cand = first_in_interval(2, 0, x - R, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand
                    cand = first_in_interval(2, x + R, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand

                # left
                C = x
                R = D - C
                if R <= 0:
                    cand = first_in_interval(3, 0, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand
                else:
                    cand = first_in_interval(3, 0, y - R, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand
                    cand = first_in_interval(3, y + R, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best: best = cand

                if best < INF:
                    nxt[i] = best
            return nxt

        def feasible(D):
            if D == 0:
                return True
            nxt = build_nxt(D)
            for s in range(n):
                cur = s
                cnt = 1
                end = s + n
                while cnt < k:
                    if cur < n:
                        nxt_idx = nxt[cur]
                    else:
                        ni = nxt[cur - n]
                        nxt_idx = ni + n if ni != -1 else -1
                    if nxt_idx == -1 or nxt_idx >= end:
                        break
                    cur = nxt_idx
                    cnt += 1
                if cnt == k:
                    if abs(xs[cur % n] - xs[s]) + abs(ys[cur % n] - ys[s]) >= D:
                        return True
            return False

        lo, hi = 0, 2 * S
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi + 1) // 2
            if feasible(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid - 1
        return lo
```

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left, bisect_right

class Solution:
    def maxDistance(self, side: int, points: List[List[int]], k: int) -> int:
        S = side
        n = len(points)
        L = 4 * S

        # Perimeter coordinate and side id.
        # sides: 0 bottom, 1 right, 2 top, 3 left
        arr = []
        for x, y in points:
            if y == 0:
                p = x
                s = 0
            elif x == S:
                p = S + y
                s = 1
            elif y == S:
                p = 3 * S - x
                s = 2
            else:  # x == 0
                p = 4 * S - y
                s = 3
            arr.append((p, x, y, s))

        arr.sort(key=lambda t: t[0])

        ps = [a[0] for a in arr]
        xs = [a[1] for a in arr]
        ys = [a[2] for a in arr]
        side_id = [a[3] for a in arr]

        side_pts = [[] for _ in range(4)]
        side_pvals = [[] for _ in range(4)]

        for i in range(n):
            s = side_id[i]
            side_pts[s].append(i)
            side_pvals[s].append(ps[i])

        INF = 10**30

        def first_in_interval(side, l, r, p_u):
            """Earliest point on this side with perimeter coordinate in [l, r]
            after p_u in circular order. Return duplicated index or INF."""
            if l < 0:
                l = 0
            if r > S:
                r = S
            if l > r:
                return INF

            if side == 0:
                pL, pR = l, r
            elif side == 1:
                pL, pR = S + l, S + r
            elif side == 2:
                pL, pR = 3 * S - r, 3 * S - l
            else:
                pL, pR = 4 * S - r, 4 * S - l

            pvals = side_pvals[side]
            pts = side_pts[side]

            idx = bisect_left(pvals, pL)
            if idx < len(pvals) and pvals[idx] <= pR:
                if pvals[idx] > p_u:
                    return pts[idx]

                idx2 = bisect_right(pvals, p_u)
                if idx2 < len(pvals) and pvals[idx2] <= pR:
                    return pts[idx2]

                return pts[idx] + n

            return INF

        def build_nxt(D):
            """nxt[i] = earliest point after i with Manhattan distance >= D."""
            nxt = [-1] * n

            for i in range(n):
                x = xs[i]
                y = ys[i]
                p_u = ps[i]
                best = INF

                # Bottom side: (t, 0), distance = |x-t| + y
                R = D - y
                if R <= 0:
                    cand = first_in_interval(0, 0, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand
                else:
                    cand = first_in_interval(0, 0, x - R, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand
                    cand = first_in_interval(0, x + R, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand

                # Right side: (S, t), distance = (S-x) + |t-y|
                C = S - x
                R = D - C
                if R <= 0:
                    cand = first_in_interval(1, 0, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand
                else:
                    cand = first_in_interval(1, 0, y - R, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand
                    cand = first_in_interval(1, y + R, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand

                # Top side: (t, S), distance = |x-t| + (S-y)
                C = S - y
                R = D - C
                if R <= 0:
                    cand = first_in_interval(2, 0, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand
                else:
                    cand = first_in_interval(2, 0, x - R, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand
                    cand = first_in_interval(2, x + R, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand

                # Left side: (0, t), distance = x + |y-t|
                C = x
                R = D - C
                if R <= 0:
                    cand = first_in_interval(3, 0, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand
                else:
                    cand = first_in_interval(3, 0, y - R, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand
                    cand = first_in_interval(3, y + R, S, p_u)
                    if cand < best:
                        best = cand

                if best < INF:
                    nxt[i] = best

            return nxt

        def feasible(D):
            if D == 0:
                return True

            nxt = build_nxt(D)

            for start in range(n):
                cur = start
                cnt = 1
                end = start + n

                while cnt < k:
                    if cur < n:
                        nxt_idx = nxt[cur]
                    else:
                        ni = nxt[cur - n]
                        nxt_idx = ni + n if ni != -1 else -1

                    if nxt_idx == -1 or nxt_idx >= end:
                        break

                    cur = nxt_idx
                    cnt += 1

                if cnt == k:
                    if abs(xs[cur % n] - xs[start]) + abs(ys[cur % n] - ys[start]) >= D:
                        return True

            return False

        lo, hi = 0, 2 * S
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi + 1) // 2
            if feasible(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid - 1

        return lo
```
